Market analysis

OIL FUTURES: Crude slides as Democratic Party midterm victory reduces likelihood of US-Iran escalation
Democrats win Senate and House control
Schumer says Iran war must end
Crude oil futures settled sharply lower Nov. 4 after sweeping Democratic Party victories in the US lessened the risk of US-Iran escalation.
NYMEX December WTI settled down $7.97/b at $88.12/b and ICE January Brent declined $8.98/b to $94.62/b.
"This war must end, and make no mistake: whenever Trump defies the will of the American people or the law, we will launch investigations, take him to court, and entangle him in legislative razor wire," Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer said Nov. 4. "The days of Trump's supplicant Senate are over."
Beginning in 2027, Democrats will control both the US Senate and the House of Representatives for the first time since 2023, following a wave of victories in US elections on Nov. 3.
"A Democratic sweep changes the political cost of continuing the Iran war," Rystad Energy Chief Economist Claudio Galimberti said in a Nov. 4 note. "With both chambers now capable of passing resolutions, holding back war funding and delaying confirmations, the administration faces a shorter political runway and greater institutional pressure to find a Hormuz deal."
The selloff left front-month WTI and Brent at the lowest level since Aug. 31.
NYMEX December RBOB tumbled 20.69 cents/gallon to $2.7286/gal and December ULSD ended the session 32.51 cents/gal lower at $4.2055/gal.
(continued on page 47)

International Crude
Dated Brent physical differential sinks to lowest since May 2020
Physical differential falls over $5/b across five sessions
Prompt overhang weighs further on differentials
Elevated freight stifles arbitrage opportunities for North Sea barrels
The Dated Brent differential has sunk to its lowest level since 2020, when global crude oil markets were reeling from the outbreak of COVID-19.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the Dated Brent physical differential at a discount of $2.885/barrel to the North Sea Dated strip, sliding $2.13/b day over day, on Nov. 4.
The differential has fallen over $5/b over five pricing sessions to reach its lowest level since May 2020, when global demand for feedstock barrels evaporated in response to lockdowns around the world.
The Dated Brent differential reflects the most competitive differential among Brent, Forties, Oseberg, Ekofisk, Troll and WTI Midland grades each day during the 10-day to one-month forward assessment period.
The ballooning supply balance within the North Sea spot market has been attributed to several factors.
An elevated freight environment has limited any potential to move North Sea barrels to Asia, with competitively priced Persian Gulf barrels also stifling the arbitrage opportunities of barrels that may clear to the major importing region.
This has also resulted in more WTI Midland volumes pointing toward Europe, with Asia taking a smaller volume of the US swing grade during the November loading cycle.
"The VLCC count ex USGC for November is materially lower than October and September," one Europe-based crude trader said, but noted that the US is also able to absorb excess barrels -- a factor that could impact the eventual flows toward Europe.
Furthermore, a well-supplied Mediterranean market is likely to push transatlantic volumes toward the North Sea, dampening differentials within Northwest Europe.
"With CPC [Blend] so cheap, WTI [Midland] into the Mediterranean is not necessary. So balance has to clear to NWE," the trader said.

A WTI Midland cargo for end-November also changed hands at a $2.95/b premium to Dated Brent, with Moeve lifting an offer from BP. The trade reflected weaker values both on the day and in trader reports from the previous week that had placed value for November barrels around premiums as low as $3/b.

Platts Dated Brent assessed at $96.16/b, below $100/b for first time in two months
ICE Brent futures retreat on bearish impact of US midterm elections
North Sea physical market sinks on oversupplied prompt balance
The Platts Dated Brent benchmark fell below the $100/b mark on Nov. 4 for the first time in two months as Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the Dated Brent benchmark at $96.16/b Nov. 4, down $9.035/b from the previous close..
The decline came on a combination of an oversupplied North Sea crude market and a retreating forward market after Democrats took control of both houses of Congress in the US midterm elections.
The benchmark was last assessed below $100/b on Sept. 2 at $98.485/b, Platts data showed.
The Dated Brent physical differential, which reflects the most competitive differential among the grades in the basket for each day of the assessment, also sank $2.13/b on the day to a discount of $2.885/b against the North Sea Dated strip Nov. 4. This was its lowest value since May 2020.
The decline in outright prices tracked a slide in the forward market through the session, with futures prices retreating as market participants assessed the impact of the US midterm elections, which saw Democrats taking control of both houses of Congress.
Platts assessed the front-month January ICE Brent futures contract at $98.53/b at the London close Nov. 4, down $5.07/b or 4.89% from its previous settlement.
"The flat price has already shown the [bearish] sentiment," said a Europe-based North Sea crude trader, commenting on the pricing impact of the elections.
Analysts noted that the election results and diplomatic progress in the Middle East have both led to an unwinding of some geopolitical risk premium in the crude futures market.
"The market increasingly appears to be betting that Trump may seek to put the Middle East conflict behind him following the election setback," said Ole Hansen, Head of Commodity Strategy at Saxo.
"Recent developments have encouraged hopes of a diplomatic breakthrough, although significant differences remain between Washington and Tehran," said Hansen.
"The new Congress could use its influence over spending to challenge additional military funding and increase pressure for

a diplomatic solution once it takes office in January," said Ipek Ozkardeskaya, Senior Analyst at Swissquote.
Further progress on talks to facilitate vessel flows through the Strait of Hormuz could also see oil prices "fall sustainably," but Ozkardeskaya cautioned that the result does not immediately entail a resolution to the ongoing conflict.
-- Natasha Tan, Ernest Puey
Middle East oil exports near wartime high in October as 230 tankers ship Gulf cargoes
Total oil exports reach 72% of pre-war level
Maritime attacks surge to 35 incidents in Oct
Tankers sail dark through Strait of Hormuz
Middle Eastern oil exports remained near a wartime high in October despite the heaviest wave of maritime attacks during the ongoing US-Iran conflict, as some 230 ships engaged in Gulf trades helped keep oil flowing to global markets, according to industry estimates.
Latest S&P Global Commodities at Sea data shows 15.2 million barrels per day of crude shipments from the Middle East last month, or 80% of the average level between December 2025 and February 2026 before the war broke out in end-February. Total exports of petroleum products reached 3.6 million b/d, or 51% of the pre-war level.

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

November 4, 2026

When crude and products were combined, October's total oil exports reached 18.8 million b/d, just off the wartime record of 18.9 million b/d in September and amounting to 72% of the prewar level, according to CAS.
The figures comprise direct shipments from Persian Gulf ports via Hormuz to non-Middle Eastern locations, loadings from other tankers in the Gulf of Oman, Fujairah and Saudi Red Sea ports. They could be revised later as more data becomes available.
JP Morgan separately estimates Middle Eastern oil flows have recovered to 20.7 million b/d currently, and that global oil markets were already in a surplus of 1.1 million b/d in September and 1.4 million b/d last month when rising supply elsewhere and weaker demand were taken into account.
"The surplus is most evident in crude and is showing up in both trade flows and inventories ... The same cannot be said for refined products," the bank said in a note Nov. 4.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed Dated Brent crude prices at $96.16 per barrel Nov. 4, up 35.6% from $70.94/b on Feb. 27. Ultra low sulfur diesel barge prices on a free-on-board Amsterdam-Rotterdam-Antwerp basis rose 79.5% to $1,350.25 per metric ton in the same period.
Gulf fleet Strong exports came despite that October was the worst
month for attacks on commercial shipping in the Middle East since the war began, with the International Maritime Organization recording 35 incidents, up from 15 in September and exceeding the previous wartime record of 20 in March.

In a note Oct. 28, risk consultancy Windward said Gulf oil producers and trading houses were using nearly 230 tankers under their control to transport barrels from Persian Gulf ports out of the Strait of Hormuz despite Iran's security threats.
"The fleet almost exclusively sails dark in the Middle East Gulf, with Automatic Identification System switched off for legitimate safety and security reasons," Windward said. "Tankers load dark, and then transit the Strait of Hormuz via Oman's southern corridor with US military support."
Windward estimates that 80% of the fleet, managed, owned, period- or spot-chartered by the parties involved, are VLCCs while 10% were Aframaxes/LR2 tankers, and that their average age is 12 years.
Separately, Iran's Persian Gulf Strait Authority on Nov. 2 expanded its list of ships allegedly violating the country's Hormuz transit rules by 114 ships, bringing the total number of listed ships to 240. The Iranian authority has previously threatened non-compliant vessels with fines, seizure or confiscation.
The ships include 46 managed by South Korea's Sinokor, the world's largest VLCC operator, as well as at least 76 owned by Saudi, Qatari, UAE and Kuwait state interests, according to Orbital Risk Intelligence.
Sinokor, Bahri, Kuwaiti Oil Tanker Co. and QatarEnergy didn't respond to emails seeking comments. ADNOC Logistics & Services said crew safety is the company's highest priority while declining to offer further comments.
-- Max Lin, Lulwa Albuainain

CHINA DATA: Independent refiners' Iranian crude imports reach record low in October; Iraqi barrels up
Iranian crude imports slump 80% YOY
Iraqi crude leads replacement barrels
China's independent refineries imported 1.16 million metric tons, or 275,000 barrels/day, of Iranian crude in October, the lowest monthly volume since Platts began collecting the data in January 2022, as supplies dried up.
October Iranian crude imports to China dropped 80% year over year and 49% from September, according to data collected by Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, and released Nov. 4.
Only five Iranian crude cargoes were discharged during October, down from 13 in September, the data showed.

The refiners imported a combined 3.12 million mt of crudes from Iraq and the UAE in October, nearly four times the 790,000 mt imported in September, according to Platts data.
Iraqi crude accounted for the bulk of the increase, with imports rising to about 2.31 million mt in October from 520,000 mt in September.
Taking into account the frequent buyers, including the private mega refineries, the independent sector imported 5.33 million mt of crude from the UAE and Iraq in October, rising from 3 million mt in September, Platts data showed.
"I think those Basrah crudes can almost offset the loss of Iranian crudes," said a trader in Shandong.
Basrah Medium crude for December delivery was offered at premiums of about $13-$14/b to ICE Brent crude futures on a DES Shandong basis Nov. 4, broadly stable from a week earlier, according to two trader sources.
-- Daisy Xu - Oil Market Specialist, Oceana Zhou - Oil Market Specialist
CRUDE MOC: Middle East differentials inch lower
7 Dubai partials trade during MOC
Trafigura offers 3 mil barrels of December Oman crude

Iranian crude flows to China's independent refining sector have declined sharply since reaching an eight-month high of 1.81 million b/d in April, as the US blockade of Iranian shipping increasingly constrained supplies.
"There have been no offers for the grade for months as there are no cargoes available anymore," a Shandong-based trader said.
Iranian crude and condensate on the water in Southeast Asia stood at 2 million barrels and 2.2 million barrels in the Far East Oct. 19, compared with a combined 56.9 million barrels Feb. 28 when the war in the Middle East started, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea data.
China's independent refiners have relied heavily on discounted Iranian crude as a source of cheap feedstock since 2022. Imports reached a record 1.91 million b/d, or 8.07 million mt, in March 2025 and averaged about 1.5 million b/d over 2025, Platts data showed.
Flows have fallen sharply since geopolitical tensions in the Middle East escalated at the end of February.
Over January-October, China imported 48.06 million mt of Iranian crude, down 23% from 62.09 million mt in the same period a year earlier, according to Platts data.
Iraqi barrels fill supply gap Small Chinese independent refiners, which heavily relied on
Iranian oil, have stepped up purchases of alternative barrels from Iraq and the UAE, Platts data showed.

Cash differentials for Middle East sour crude markers inched lower during the Singapore Platts Market on Close assessment process Nov. 4, while selling interest for December Oman crude was seen.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed January cash Dubai, Oman and Murban all at a premium of 64 cents/b to same-month Dubai futures at the market close, down 28 cents/b on the day.
During the MOC, 7 January Dubai partials of 25,000 barrels each traded. The sellers were Mercuria, Trafigura, TotalEnergies and ExxonMobil, while the buyers were BP and Vitol.
Trafigura was seen offering 3 million barrels in total of December-loading Oman crude during the Nov. 4 MOC. It had offered a 2 million barrel clip, with the offer left standing at a $4/b premium to Platts December Dubai crude assessments, FOB, as well as a second 1 million barrel clip, with that offer left standing at a $3.95/b premium to Platts December Dubai crude assessments, FOB.

Vopak sees Europoort oil storage staying strong into 2027
Rotterdam occupancy hits 95% in Q3
LNG Gate terminal expands to 20 Bcm/year
EBITDA guidance raised amid strong outlook
Vopak expects strong performance at its 24 million-barrel Europoort oil storage hub in Rotterdam to continue into 2027 after strong oil markets support its terminal business this year, the company said Nov. 4.
In its quarterly report, the Netherlands-based terminal operator reported its Dutch occupancy rate rose to 95% in the third quarter from 93% in Q2. The company said its global oil hubs continued to perform strongly.
Speaking during Vopak's third-quarter earnings call, CFO Michiel Gilsing identified Europoort, one of Rotterdam's main oil storage facilities, as a significant contributor to the company's performance.
"The performance across the existing network was strong, primarily driven by strong oil markets, which benefited, for example, our Europoort oil hub terminal in the Netherlands and oil distribution terminals in South Africa," Gilsing said.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the barge price for ultra low sulfur diesel with 10 parts-per-million sulfur limits at $1,421.50 per metric ton Nov. 3, up 132.75% from $610.75/mt Jan. 2, on a free-on-board Amsterdam-Rotterdam-Antwerp basis.
Asked about the prospects for 2027, Gilsing said the outlook was "effectively the same" as the company's strong South African oil-storage business.
"If we look at the contracts we have in place, if we look at the renewal risks, obviously, anything may happen. But so far, if we look at it, then it should be positive," Gilsing said.
Company-wide demand for terminal capacity remained firm. Vopak reported proportional occupancy for its tankers globally of 91% in January-September, roughly flat on the year, with Gilsing saying demand for the company's services "remained strong."
In Singapore, another major oil storage and trading center, occupancy was 92% in the third quarter, compared with 93% in the previous three months, according to the company's presentation.
Vopak executives also pushed back against suggestions that stronger oil-storage performance could prompt it to dispose of assets as it shifts investment toward gas and energy-transition infrastructure.
Vopak had been encouraged to sell oil assets several years ago because of expectations of declining oil demand, Gilsing said, but the company took a different view.
"I don't want to... say that there is no risk in oil, but definitely, there is also a bit of lifetime in oil," he said.
Well-located oil terminals could also be repurposed to store low-carbon fuels as the energy mix evolves. Vopak has "no plan at the moment" to sell its oil assets, although it continues to review its portfolio, Gilsing said.

LNG, LPG projects In LNG, a fourth 180,000-cu-m tank at the Gate terminal in
Rotterdam started operations Oct. 1, lifting total regasification capacity to 20 Bcm/year, according to Vopak. The capacity is backed by long-term take-or-pay contracts.
CEO Dick Richelle said the expanded terminal could provide gas equivalent to around two-thirds of Dutch consumption. Vopak is also constructing a fourth jetty at Gate, expected to start small-scale LNG loading and bunkering by the end of 2028.
In Canada, Phase 1 of the REEF LPG export terminal remained on track for commissioning in Q1 2027, while a further optimization adding propane and butane storage and railunloading capacity is being considered.
Richelle said the terminal would provide Canadian producers with a direct route to Asia, reducing sailing times to around 10 days compared with 25 days from the US Gulf Coast, offering "significant cost advantages."
However, some newer energy-transition infrastructure is taking longer to develop.
"Anything which is related to ammonia and hydrogen or CO2 storage is harder to get off the ground," Gilsing said, adding such investments were now likely "closer to the end of the decade."
Earnings outlook raised Vopak's proportional EBITDA reached 906 million ($1.02
billion) in January-September, a 5% increase on a like-for-like basis, while operating free cash flow rose to  671 million.
The company raised the lower end of its 2026 proportional EBITDA guidance from 1.18 billion to 1.20 billion, giving a range of Eur1.20 billion-Eur1.22 billion, while proportional operating free cash flow is expected at around 840 million versus 820 million previously.
Gilsing said Vopak expected "a step-up in terms of growth" in 2027 as new projects begin contributing to earnings.
-- Max Lin
UK introduces legislation to lower energy bills, end new North Sea oil licenses
Energy Independence Bill laid before Parliament
Focuses on consumer relief, ending oil licensing
Changes will 'bring bills down': Burnham
The UK government introduced sweeping energy market legislation to Parliament on Nov. 4, laying the groundwork for lowering consumer energy bills and ending new North Sea oil and gas exploration licenses.
The Energy Independence Bill combines near-term consumer relief with structural changes to grid infrastructure, renewable power and offshore oil and gas licensing, according to a news release.
Building on measures announced in the UK's Oct. 28 budget, the government said it will bring forward amendments to the bill to support plans to scrap the daily standing charge on household

gas bills, a fixed cost levied regardless of consumption. Around 23 million households would be affected, with
approximately 60% of gas account holders expected to save an average of £45 a year, the government said.
Affordability is a central focus for UK Prime Minister Andy Burnham, who said the legislation is designed to address structural vulnerabilities in Britain's energy system.
"For too long, families have paid the price for an energy system that isn't working for them. Bills are too high and events around the world can send costs soaring," the prime minister said in a statement. "Together, these changes will bring bills down for good, give families greater security and put Britain back in control of its energy future."
Among other measures, the bill gives UK energy regulator Ofgem greater powers to protect consumers from "rogue" energy companies and mis-selling, which are expected to save businesses around £100 million per year, the government said.
The government added that it would bring forward a further amendment to enshrine in law Great British Grid, the UK's new publicly owned investment body for electricity networks. Separately, the bill extends an existing policy -- moving 75% of domestic renewables obligation costs from energy bills onto general taxation -- through March 2029.
Elsewhere, the government said the bill will give it powers to update existing electricity market rules, network charging and trading arrangements, as part of its commitment to deliver "reformed national pricing."
The government also announced requirements for renewable energy developers to offer communities up to a 20% stake in new projects, allowing local people to benefit directly from clean energy.
"Today is a first down payment on fixing a broken system, protecting consumers from unfair costs and putting the public back in control," Energy Secretary Miatta Fahnbulleh said in the statement. "Through this bill, we will build a clean energy future which people feel in their pockets, households and communities, ensuring support reaches those who need it most."
Oil, gas transition Beyond consumer measures, the Energy Independence Bill
also formalizes the end of new licenses for oil and gas exploration in the UK's North Sea -- a Labour Party manifesto commitment now being enshrined in law.
The government has sought to limit near-term supply disruption by introducing new Transitional Energy Certificates, which would allow continued and expanded production from already-explored areas, including through existing infrastructure.
The government said these so-called tie-back developments could unlock around 60 million barrels of oil and support hundreds of jobs, providing a managed pathway for operators with existing licensed acreage. The North Sea Transition Authority's objectives and powers would also be updated under the bill to reflect its expanded stewardship role during the energy transition.

As part of its North Sea strategy, the government recently pledged a £2 billion Energy Wealth Fund and a North Sea Jobs Service to support workers and investment in transition industries.
The bill would also end new licenses for onshore oil and gas in England, including those that could be used for shale-gas fracking, and extend the UK's core fuels regulatory regime to midstream gas and LPG, giving the government powers to direct operators to address supply resilience risks and, in rare cases, provide financial support, it said.

GME Oman crude futures Oct traded volumes rise 3.5% MOM
Trading volume totaled 179,136 lots in Oct Open interest jumps 13.51% by month-end Cash Oman prices fall to $110.82/barrel

The total traded volume of GME Oman crude oil futures in October rose 3.53% month over month to 179,136 lots, Gulf Mercantile Exchange data showed Nov. 4.
All contracts traded on the GME were Oman crude futures. The GME also lists other crude futures contracts, including Oman crude financial futures and the Brent-Oman futures spread.
Open interest for the GME Oman crude futures contract stood at 17,312 lots Oct. 30, up 13.51% from the end of September when open interest closed the month at 15,252 lots.
GME Oman futures is a physically delivered futures contract for the Oman crude blend.
Platts cash Oman averaged $110.82/barrel in October, down from $113.77/b in September, while its spread against samemonth Dubai futures averaged a premium of $8.18/b in October, down from $21.65/b in September.
Fundamentals in the Middle East crude complex had weakened last month amid ample Middle East crude offers and sales by both producers and traders, while refinery run cuts had been made in parts of Asia as high oil prices and freight rates ate into domestic margins.
December-loading or January-arrival cargoes of Oman crude meanwhile had been heard sold to various buyers across the region, including those in Japan, Taiwan and Malaysia, Platts reported earlier.

ICE Dubai crude futures traded volume declines MOM in October
Traded volume at 1.79 mil lots in October
Cash-futures spread plunges from $21/b to $8/b
Refinery cuts in Asia reduce crude demand
The volume of ICE Dubai crude futures traded on the Intercontinental Exchange dropped 26.25% month over month to 1.79 million lots in October, exchange data showed Nov. 4, as ample spot offers and poor demand drove regional cash differentials to multi-month lows last month.
ICE Dubai crude futures comprise Dubai first-line futures, Dated Brent versus Dubai first-line futures and Brent first-line versus Dubai first-line futures. One lot is equivalent to 1,000 barrels.
The lower traded volume came as Dubai first-line futures volume fell 29.93% month over month to 1.12 million lots, and Brent first-line versus Dubai first-line futures volume dropped 21.27% to 643,623 lots, even as Dated Brent versus Dubai first-line futures volume rose 99.37% to 28,757 lots, exchange data showed.
The fall in volumes comes amid weaker fundamentals for the Middle East sour crude complex in October. Ample Middle East crude offers and sales had been made by both producers and traders last month, while refinery run cuts were heard taking place in parts of Asia as high oil prices and freight rates ate into domestic margins.
December cash Dubai assessments averaged $ 110.73/barrel in October, down from an average of $113.711//b for front-month November cash Dubai in September, while the Dubai cash-futures spread -- a measure of the market structure -- averaged $8.10/b in October, down from $21.60/b in September, data from Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, showed.
That weakness has lasted into the new January-loading trade cycle, with the Platts Dubai cash-futures spread plummeting to a three-month low premium of 30 cents/b on the first trading day of the month, Nov. 2. Trade sources had noted an overhang of unsold cargoes from the prior cycle lasting into the new month.
Meanwhile, combined open interest in Dubai crude futures totaled 772,363 lots on Oct 30, down from 901,156 lots at the end of September, according to exchange data.
The lower combined open interest across Dubai crude futures came as Dubai first-line futures open interest dropped 19.48% month over month to 404,908 lots and Brent firstline versus Dubai first-line futures open interest fell 12.46% to 326,482 lots, even as Dated Brent versus Dubai first-line futures open interest rose 61.65% to 40,973 lots, exchange data showed.
Open interest in the front-month November Dubai crude futures contract on ICE totaled 189,036 lots at the end of October, down 15.91% month over month, according to exchange data.

Platts Asia Pacific Sweet Crude Daily Market Analysis
Closed arbitrage limits US crude flows to Asia Lower freight could support heavy sweet crude premiums China's independent refiners' Iranian crude imports plunge

The Asia-Pacific sweet crude market on Nov. 4 saw laggard trading activity on the back of a closed arbitrage window from the West, while weakening freight rates could support premiums of heavy, sweet crude grades, despite refining margins dipping.
"It makes no sense in economics [to procure the US WTI Midland grade currently," an Asia-based crude trader said, with another Singapore-based crude trader noting that the last trading activity of the grade had been when Indian refiner BPCL was heard to have procured 1 million barrels of US crude WTI Midland - for arrival over late December to early January -- from Occidental Petroleum, which had occurred late last week.
The cargoes had changed hands at a premium of $18/b over December Dated Brent crude assessments, DAP Mumbai, according to several Singapore-based crude trade sources, Platts reported previously.
Meanwhile, a Europe-based crude trader noted that weakening VLCC rates from West Africa to the Far East could potentially support FOB premiums of Sudan/South Sudan's Nile Blend crudes and Dar Blend crudes. The VLCC rate from West Africa-Far East route had dived $85.69/mt, or 34.7% to $161.22/mt on Nov. 3.
"Freight came off, premiums should [hence] be a bit higher as well, think the freight component has a larger impact this time," the trader added, offsetting the drop in refining margins seen by the heavy distillates.
The Platts-assessed second-month low sulfur fuel oil swap crack against Dubai crude averaged $10.05/b month-to-date Nov. 4, compared to an average of $15.25/b in October.

Similarly, the second-month 180 CST HSFO and 380 CST HSFO swap cracks against Dubai crude averaged minus 37 cents/b and minus $1.43/b, respectively, month to date through Nov. 4, compared with October averages of $3.01/b and $1.87/b, Platts data showed.
On the condensate front, January-loading barrels of Australia's North West Shelf condensate were heard valued at parity to small premiums to Platts Dated Brent crude assessments, FOB, a Singapore-based trader said, while January-loading Wheatstone condensate barrels was heard valued at a premium of $5/b to the same benchmark.
Elsewhere, China's independent refineries imported 1.16 million metric tons, or 275,000 barrels/day, of Iranian crude in October, the lowest monthly volume since Platts began collecting the data in January 2022, as supplies dried up.
October Iranian crude imports to China plunged 80% year over year and 49% from September, according to data collected by Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, and released Nov. 4.
Only five Iranian crude cargoes were discharged during October, down from 13 in September, the data showed.
Iranian crude flows to China's independent refining sector have declined sharply since reaching an eight-month high of 1.81 million b/d in April, as the US blockade of Iranian shipping increasingly constrained supplies.
Crude oil futures were rangebound to lower in Asian afternoon trade Nov. 4, as signs of progress in US-Iran diplomacy weighed on prices.
Reports that Iran's Supreme Leader Mojtaba Khamenei granted negotiators greater authority to discuss previously nonnegotiable issues raised hopes of further diplomatic progress, although disagreements over Tehran's nuclear program, sanctions relief and the US naval blockade remained.
Meanwhile, markets continued to assess the US midterm election results, with expectations of a Democratic Senate victory raising the prospect of a divided Congress that could constrain President Donald Trump's fiscal agenda, according to Priyanka Sachdeva, Head, Market Insights at Phillip Novaa Nov. 4.
Additionally, the American Petroleum Institute last reported a 5.267-million-barrel draw in US crude oil inventories in the week ended Oct. 30, surpassing expectations of a 2.1 million barrel-draw and marking the fifth consecutive week of stock drawdowns.
Platts is part of S&P Global Energy -- Alvinn Philips

Platts Mediterranean & Black Sea Sweet Crude Daily Commentary
Traders wait for December trading to begin
CPC still depressed, looking for NWE arbitrage
The crude oil market in the Mediterranean was sluggish Nov. 4, with some participants still looking for late-November cargoes as market volatility compressed refinery procurement cycles this year.
"Most refineries would buy their crude around two months in advance, but for the past few months this has been compressed to more like three to five weeks," one Mediterranean trader said. "With premiums evolving rapidly, buyers prefer to buy more barrels in the spot market, and that's why today few people are trading cargoes loading in December."
The market remained quiet, pending the start of December trading. Some market participants said refineries may pay higher prices for higher-quality grades in December, as they respond to increased end-user demand for oil products as the market exits the November shoulder season.
But while sources agreed that demand for oil products should pick up, potentially widening cracks even further, the willingness

of refiners to pay more for certain grades will depend on which grades they use.
"Azeri Light below $10/b on CIF Augusta basis could be supportive for diesel cracks but whether a refiner would pay up for grades with higher diesel yields depends on the set up and how much Azeri you actually run," a second Med source said.
A contango in prompt North Sea CFDs would indicate a more constructive market in December, but not all grades may benefit from it.
CPC Blend, which relies on arbitrages to clear monthly volumes, would have to stay low to compete with falling differentials for delivered WTI Midland.
"CPC needs to be at a level where it can arb into Northwest Europe, which is around the current level," a third Med trader said. "I am a bear at the moment based on what I see on Dated Brent, and that includes the movements in WTI Midland."
Platts is part of S&P Global Energy. -- Jan Kedzior
Platts North Sea Crude Daily Market Analysis
Sweet, sour segments both decline on oversupply
Forward market retreats following US midterms
Sustained oversupply within the North Sea crude oil market piled pressure on differentials in the region Nov. 4.
The prompt oversupply appeared to drag on values across both sweet and sour segments during the session.
The Platts Market on Close assessment process saw an early offer by Shell for a Flotta Gold cargo loading over Nov. 23-25.
The offer, floated at 0844 GMT at a $1.80/b discount to Dated Brent, was withdrawn at 1106 GMT at a $6.60/b discount.
A BP offer for a Johan Sverdrup cargo loading over Nov. 20-22 was left standing at the London close at a discount of $3/b to Dated Brent, tracking the weakness demonstrated by Flotta Gold.
The sweet crude market was not spared, with offers for Forties and Oseberg cargoes returning and demonstrating weaker values on the day.
A WTI Midland cargo changed hands at a $2.95/b premium to Dated Brent, with Moeve lifting the bp offer before the close. The trade reflected weaker values on the day.
The December supply balance remained in flux, with traders expressing conflicting views on the direction of premiums for the month.
While demand is anticipated to hold firm amid limited downstream product stocks, the ample supply of CPC Blend, which continues to struggle to find homes, is likely to dampen demand for WTI Midland barrels in the Mediterranean.
"With CPC [Blend] so cheap, WTI [Midland] into the Mediterranean is not necessary. So balance has to clear to [Northwest Europe]," said one Europe-based crude trader.
Much will depend on the ability of US swing barrels to move toward Asia, as prohibitive freight rates are limiting the arbitrage potential for moving volumes to the East.

More broadly, the flat price environment saw a choppy session following the US midterm elections, which saw Democrats take control of both houses of Congress.
Platts assessed the front-month January ICE Brent futures contract at $98.53/b at the London close Nov. 4, down $5.07/b or 4.89% from its previous settlement.
"The flat price has already shown the [bearish] sentiment," said the second trader.
Platts is part of S&P Global Energy. -- Ernest Puey
Platts West African Crude Daily Commentary
Easing freight rates, structure provide some relief
Competition from Atlantic Basin remains an obstacle
The West African crude complex saw muted trading activity Nov. 4, with traders still expecting further declines in differentials despite large movements across freight rates and paper structure.
Recent pricing sessions have seen freight rates on routes between West Africa, Europe and Asia easing significantly from the historical highs seen in October, easing some pressure on FOB differentials in the region.
Participants have also noted a narrowing backwardation across the Brent CFD contracts settling across November and December -- with some prompt weeks having flipped into a contango -- easing the logistics associated with long-haul journeys.
"Freight and structure is collapsing, and that's tempting people back into the market, so some of the reasons for the impasse have been dealt with," said a Europe-based WAF crude trader.
However, a well-supplied Atlantic basin continues to create a difficult environment in which to clear cargoes, particularly for Nigerian grades pointing toward Europe.
Market participants continued to note the overhang and collapsing values across the North Sea and Mediterranean crude complex as bearish indicators for unsold West African cargoes competing within the light sweet crude segment.
"Freight is coming off, of course, and CFD structure should help put some value back into FOB levels, but I think that's already factored in," said a second WAF trader. "There is scant evidence of better demand. It may be the case for Angolans, but as usual Nigerian needs to clear into the Atlantic basin and there's clearly an over-supply situation there."
While East-facing Angolan and regional medium heavy grades could see more outlets available, competition from within the Atlantic basin could keep pressure on differentials.

"Angolan demand isn't great -- competing Brazilian cargoes seem to be heading east," said a third trader.
Indeed, a December-loading cargo of Congolese crude Djeno was heard to have traded around a $30/b discount to Dated Brent -- generally unchanged from where indications for November-loaders had been reported over the previous weeks.
Platts is part of S&P Global Energy. -- Oliwier Wronski

Rosenthal wins Texas oil, gas regulator seat, first for a Democrat since 1995
Former engineer pushes stricter utility oversight
Targets reduced flaring and orphan wells
Democrat Jon Rosenthal won the Nov. 3 election for the vacant commissioner's seat at the Texas Railroad Commission, defeating Bo French (Republican) and Arthur DiBianca (Libertarian).
The Railroad Commission (RRC), despite its name, regulates oil and gas drilling, pipeline safety, natural gas utilities, surface mining and underground injection control in the country's largest oilproducing state. No Democrat had won an RRC seat since 1995.
The Associated Press called the race for Rosenthal early Nov. 4. Rosenthal led French 52.6% to 45.5%. DiBianca had received 1.9%, with 93% of the vote completed.
Rosenthal is a four-term state representative in the Texas Legislature and a career oilfield mechanical engineer.
Rosenthal's influence on the commission will be limited since the RRC's three-person body structure gives each commissioner one vote. He will join two industry-aligned Republicans, Christi Craddick and Wayne Christian.
For gas utilities, Rosenthal's priorities include stricter prudence reviews on utilities to cover costs and greater transparency in gas utility billing.
For wells, his priorities include reducing flaring, "turbocharging" orphan-well plugging, and raising bonding requirements for new drilling permits. He also promotes job training to support small operators with technical assistance in installing capture and compression, as well as new technologies such as geothermal and carbon dioxide storage.
Rosenthal would like to add community liaisons in every region; upgrade pipeline safety; and require site-specific emergency plans, alarms and public notification for releases of sour gas and hazardous chemicals.

Rosenthal was endorsed by the Sierra Club, an environmental organization, and the Texas AFL-CIO, a labor federation.
French, the former Tarrant County Republican Party chairman, focused his campaign on topics such as his opposition to diversity, equity and inclusion programs, rather than the agency's core regulatory portfolio.

Benson wins Michigan governor race as Line 5 pipeline uncertainty persists
Line 5 pipeline legal battles to continue
Democrat pledges Great Lakes protection audit
Democrat Jocelyn Benson has won the race to become Michigan's governor, according to unofficial election results Nov. 3, meaning the future of Enbridge Inc.'s Line 5 oil pipeline remains uncertain as the state continues years-long legal and regulatory proceedings.
Benson defeated Republican John James and will succeed Democrat Gretchen Whitmer, who served the maximum two terms as governor. The Associated Press called the race for Benson at 1:45 am ET on Nov. 4, when she led James 54.4% to 43.9%, with 95% of the vote completed.
Benson has not explicitly endorsed the Whitmer administration's campaign to shut down Line 5. She also has not stated a position on Enbridge's proposed Great Lakes Tunnel, a project that has pitted the Canadian energy company against environmental opposition.
Benson did not address Line 5 directly during her campaign. Instead, she pledged to "explore every option to ensure we are protecting our Great Lakes and diversifying Michigan's energy sources" in an August statement to the media outlet Bridge Michigan.
Her environmental agenda called for auditing aging pipelines statewide and developing a plan to replace any infrastructure found to be hazardous or polluting, "including Line 5."
The outcome of the election leaves open the prospect that Michigan will maintain an adversarial posture toward Enbridge. In 2018, a tugboat anchor accident in the Straits of Mackinac caused minor damage to a portion of the pipeline and sparked local outcry over the risk of an oil spill. The incident spurred Enbridge and then-Michigan Governor Rick Snyder to agree to replace a 4.5-mile underwater segment of the line, a project that has remained mired in legal and permitting limbo.
Under Whitmer, the state attorney general sued in 2019 to invalidate Line 5's 1953 Straits of Mackinac easement, attempting to stop the pipeline from operating altogether. The US Supreme Court ruled unanimously in April that Enbridge's attempt to move that case to federal court was untimely, returning the shutdown litigation to state court. The company continues to await judgments and permit approvals to begin the tunnel.
James had pledged to end the state's litigation against Enbridge and continue his support for the project.

Beyond Line 5, Benson has promised tighter oversight of Michigan utilities, a statewide grid audit, revised utility ratesetting, increased outage compensation, residential energyefficiency assistance and continued investment in clean energy and union jobs.
-- Kate Winston, Eamonn Brennan
Ovintiv sees runway for light oil output growth in Western Canada
Logan assets produce 7,000 b/d of crude oil
Permian Q3 output stands at 235,00 boe/d
Denver-based shale producer Ovintiv Inc. has the opportunity to increase light oil and condensate production in the Montney Basin in Western Canada following its recent acquisition of a fellow producer, CEO Brendan McCracken said Nov. 4.
"We announced last week that we have entered into an agreement to acquire Logan Energy, which adds to our top-tier position in the Montney oil window," McCracken said on the company's third-quarter 2026 earnings webcast. "The transaction is priced right, it adds significant inventory in the heart of one of our two core plays, and we expect it to create exceptional value for our shareholders."
On Oct. 26, Ovintiv unveiled a definitive agreement to buy Logan Energy Corp. under a C$1.26 billion ($890 million) deal, stating then that it will add nearly 20,000 barrels/day of oil equivalent production, including 7,000 b/d of crude oil and condensate, 70 million cubic feet/day of natural gas, and over 1 million b/d of other NGLs such as C2 (ethane) and C4 (butane).
Terming the deal as "immediately accretive" on all financial metrics, McCracken said it comes with a deep inventory of drilling locations and substantial oil-rich production.
"It also brings valuable infrastructure ownership and spare midstream capacity, which adds to our future growth optionality and increases margins. The Logan assets sit in the Montney oil window that are largely undeveloped, adding approximately 420 net 10,000-foot equivalent well locations across 200,000 net core acres," he said.
Besides substantially increasing Ovintiv's footprint in the Western Canadian Sedimentary Basin, the acquisition also offers attractive rates for development, McCracken said.
"We are acquiring this high-quality inventory at $1.5 million per premium undeveloped well location. This drops to $0.8 million per location if we include the upside inventory and highlights our ability to identify and opportunistically add inventory with superior full-cycle return propositions relative to other recent market transactions in both the Lower 48 and the Montney," he said.
For its part, Ovintiv is expected to deploy a rig to the Logan acreage, bringing its total Montney rig count to seven, McCracken said, adding that the company will evaluate the possibility of adding a second rig as it finalizes its 2027 capital plans.

SM Energy holds oil output steady despite Uinta constraints
Q4 oil output forecast at 229,000-239,000 b/d
Merger synergies reach $400M, double target
Sand mine cuts proppant costs 40% by 2027
SM Energy expects oil production to average between 229,000 barrel per day and 239,000 b/d in the fourth quarter, after producing 230,000 b/d in the third quarter, despite ongoing takeaway constraints from its Uinta assets, company executives said Nov. 4.

Overall production is expected to average between 435,000 barrels of oil equivalent per day and 445,000 boe/d in the fourth quarter, up from 433,000 boe/d in the third quarter, according to the company's earnings presentation.
Third quarter oil production was about 2% below the midpoint of the company's guidance for the quarter, Blake McKenna, SM Energy executive vice president and COO, said on the company's earnings call.
This was in part due to compliance work on acquired assets in the Permian that is now complete, and takeaway constraints in the Uinta that are expected to continue until the first quarter of 2027, McKenna said.
"Together, these constraints reduced third quarter oil volumes by approximately 4,000 barrels per day," McKenna said. "Absent them, oil production would have been approximately 234,000 barrels per day."
SM Energy has decreased companywide cycle times -- the average number of days from spud to first sales -- by 14% since the first quarter of 2026, the presentation said. In the Delaware Basin, well productivity increased 13% in 2025 and 24% in 2026, the presentation said.
Synergies from SM Energy's merger with Civitas Resources in January have exceeded expectations, said Elizabeth McDonald, president and CEO.
"At the merger announcement, we established a $200 million synergy target. Today, we have actioned approximately $400 million, twice the original target and surpassing the previously raised $375 million target," McDonald said.
The company's synergies and efficiency gains have largely offset inflation related to higher diesel unit costs, fuel-related transportation costs and other consumables, McKenna said.
A local sand mine investment in the Denver-Julesburg Basin is expected to supply all sand needs and lower proppant costs by 40% compared to the in-basin market by mid-2027, the presentation said.
That investment is expected to generate meaningful savings in the first and second quarter of 2027, McKenna said. "You'll see some of that reelected in our results as we go into next year, but sand is a very substantial portion of our costs when it comes to frac."
-- Kate Winston

What could come next for the US oil market following the Democratic Party sweep?
Does Trump make a deal or escalate with Iran? Congress likely to investigate Venezuela deals Oversight into US leasing plans expected

Oil futures fell sharply on Nov. 4 after Democratic victories in the Nov. 3 US midterm elections were seen as lowering the risk of US-Iran escalation.
At 1747 GMT, NYMEX December WTI was down $8.39/b at $87.70/b, and ICE January Brent was down $9.58/b at $94.02/b.

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

14

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

November 4, 2026

NYMEX December RBOB fell 20.59 cents/gal to $2.7296/gal and December ULSD dropped 34.04 cents/gal to $4.1902/gal.
"A Democratic sweep changes the political cost of continuing the Iran war," Rystad Energy Chief Economist Claudio Galimberti said in a Nov. 4 note. "With both chambers now capable of passing resolutions, holding back war funding and delaying confirmations, the administration faces a shorter political runway and greater institutional pressure to find a Hormuz deal."
Below are some of the implications of a Democrat-controlled Congress for the US oil industry.
Iran deal or escalation
The outcome depends on whether a Democratic Congress pressures US President Donald Trump toward a deal with Iran.
Analysts have warned Trump could escalate in the lame-duck period, and a third carrier strike group gives him that option.
The US Energy Information Administration expects Persian Gulf shut-ins to continue well into 2027, so any escalation would hit a tight market while a settlement could quickly push prices lower.
The EIA forecasts shut-in production among Kuwait, United Arab Emirates, Iran, Iraq, Qatar, Bahrain, and Saudi Arabia will average around 4.47 million barrels per day during Q4 2026, falling to 2.68 million b/d during Q1 2027.
Congress will likely pass a continuing resolution after the election and push off the budget until January or February of next year, Senator Chris Murphy (Democrat-Connecticut), a member of the Foreign Relations and Appropriations Committee, said at an Oct. 28 MEI event.
Venezuela scrutiny
Venezuela's oil sector is reopening with US government involvement, and analysts do not expect Congress to change the administration's course.
The question is whether investigations, disclosure demands and legal costs will cool investment from smaller players.
PDVSA and partners produced 1.23 million b/d of oil in October. PDVSA is targeting 1.3 million b/d by the end of 2026 and 1.5 million b/d by the end of 2027.
Analysts expect a Democratic Congress to scrutinize the US deal with North America Blue Energy Partners (NABEP), including how the company was chosen. Under the deal, signed in August, the US holds a 35% interest and has preferential access to 20% of production at cost.
On Sept. 30, Democratic Senators Jeanne Shaheen, Jack Reed, Martin Heinrich, and Elizabeth Warren wrote to the Trump administration demanding information about the deal.
Rice University senior director of the Center for Energy Studies Kenneth Medlock said hearings could chase off smaller players, while larger companies will decide on long-term risk and profitability.

Leasing oversight Analysts expect little near-term effect on US oil output, which
hit 13.97 million b/d in August, according to the EIA. Political uncertainty could weigh on longer-horizon projects sanctioned in 2027-28. A Democrat-controlled Congress will likely investigate the oil industry's dealings with the White House. Experts expect committees to start with Trump's Executive Order 14154: Unleashing American Energy, which directed the Interior Department to expand leasing on federal lands and waters, then write to companies seeking documents and communications. Legislation would still need Trump's signature, and analysts say Democrats cannot stop or meaningfully slow the current leasing program for at least two years. The Bureau of Land Management has generated over $5 billion in onshore leasing receipts in 2026, and the 2025 reconciliation law requires at least 30 Gulf lease sales through 2040. The Arctic National Wildlife Refuge and offshore expansion are the most likely points of friction with a Democratic majority, said Robert Glicksman, a professor of environmental law at George Washington University Law School.
EV and fuels policy S&P Global Energy CERA expects a Democratic Congress to
gain a larger platform to promote electric vehicles through hearings and investigations. It does not expect a meaningful acceleration of US EV adoption before the 2028 election. Democrats could use hearings and debate on the permitting bill to argue that renewables are becoming cheaper than oil and gas and that more fossil-fuel leasing locks in higher-cost energy. Restoring federal incentives would need Trump's signature or veto-proof majorities that Congress does not have. The 2025 reconciliation law ended federal clean-vehicle tax credits and cut CAFE noncompliance penalties to zero. The new CAFE rule requires 34.9 miles/gal in model year 2031, versus 50.4 miles/gal under the previous Biden-era rule. CERA projects US gasoline demand will rise to 8.9 million b/d in the rest of 2026, after averaging 94,000 b/d below the 20232025 average so far this year.
-- Staff

AOC's assets are located close to Cenovus' existing Christina Lake, May River and Thornbury assets, expanding the latter's land position in a core resource fairway and will also be a strategic fit with the core oil sands business, the release said.

The company's synthetic crude oil (SCO) output decreased to 528,100 b/d, with upgrader utilization at 98%, in the third quarter of 2026, compared to 544,100 b/d and 102%, respectively, in the prior-year quarter, primarily due to planned upgrader maintenance activities at Syncrude, it said.
SCO produced by upgrading bitumen at an upgrader fetches a premium over WTI in North American refining markets.
"Our third quarter involved completing major maintenance for the year, positioning for a strong finish and maximizing value in today's business environment," CEO Rich Kruger said on the earnings webcast Nov. 4.
Suncor's overall production -- that includes oil sands, SCO and non-oil sands -- last quarter was 853,000 b/d, which was achieved despite a 47-day turnaround of the coker at Syncrude completed in October, Kruger said.

Talos prepares broader offshore drilling, development push in 2027
Talos targets Mexico FID decisions in 2027
US Gulf output starts from two fields
Daenerys appraisal drilling continues
Talos Energy is preparing a broader offshore investment push in 2027, with new US Gulf production, investment decisions in Mexico's developments, and additional appraisal and exploration drilling across its portfolio, management said Nov. 4.
The company expects first oil from two US Gulf developments during 2027 while advancing potential final investment decisions on two major Mexican offshore projects and continuing appraisal of its Daenerys discovery.
"We have a deeper portfolio of development, appraisal, and exploration opportunities than Talos has ever had," CFO Zach Dailey said during the company's third-quarter earnings call.
Mexico projects approach FID In Mexico, Talos is working toward expected 2027 final
investment decisions for both the Zama development and Repsol-operated Block 29.
At Block 29, Talos and Repsol are progressing through concept selection toward front-end engineering and design, with a final investment decision targeted for the second half of 2027, CEO Paul Goodfellow said.
Block 29 contains two oil discoveries holding more than 200 million barrels of oil equivalent of gross recoverable resources, while Talos said the block also contains a similar level of exploration potential.
The partners are considering an additional exploration well toward the end of 2027 that could expand the resource base available for development.
Talos is awaiting approval from Mexico's energy ministry to complete its farm-in to Block 29, with Goodfellow saying the regulatory process was progressing as planned.
The company is separately working with the operator of Zama toward an expected final investment decision in 2027.
US Gulf production additions In the US Gulf, Talos expects first oil from the Monument
development in the first quarter of 2027, followed by initial production from an expanded Brutus development program in the second quarter.
Production from Brutus is expected to be phased in through the fourth quarter as additional wells come online.
Talos expanded the program from four to five wells after identifying an additional sidetrack opportunity and modified its development plan to incorporate batch drilling and completions, which the company expects to improve execution efficiency and project returns.

The company is also preparing additional opportunities around US Gulf assets acquired in September alongside partner Ridgewood.
The acquisition gave Talos a 50% working interest and operatorship in the Coulomb field, as well as a 25% nonoperated interest in the BP-operated Na Kika platform and four associated fields.
Daenerys appraisal to continue Talos Energy plans further appraisal of the Daenerys discovery
in 2027, including a sidetrack from an existing well to assess reservoir extent and fluid properties. The company is evaluating development options ranging from a tieback to existing infrastructure to a standalone facility.
Talos is also evaluating nearby prospects acquired in the December 2025 federal offshore lease sale, with an exploration well possible in late 2027 or early 2028. A discovery could potentially be developed alongside Daenerys.
CEO Paul Goodfellow said first oil from Daenerys in 2029 would be "highly unlikely," although not impossible, and would require a tieback development rather than a standalone host.
Farther south, Talos completed its initial deepwater 3D seismic acquisition program offshore Honduras in October, less than three months after signing agreements covering the acreage.
The company expects to receive the first processed seismic data in early 2027.
Talos said its broader Honduras position is equivalent in size to roughly 700 US Gulf offshore blocks, while the existing Main Cape oil discovery provides evidence of a working petroleum system in the underexplored basin.
-- Sheky Espejo
ARGENTINA DATA: September oil exports slip 6% on year to 231,564 b/d
Shipments to pick up on takeaway expansion
September gas exports tumble 29% on year
Argentina's oil exports slipped 5.9% year over year to 231,564 b/d in September, while natural gas exports tumbled 29% to 2.84 million cu m/d over the same period, an industry report showed Nov. 4.
September oil exports were down 8% from 251,591 b/d in August, according to the report by the Argentina Oil and Gas Institute.
The decline in exports came even as Argentina's total oil production rose 15.2% year over year to 961,449 b/d in September and rose 3.2% from August, the data showed.
The production growth was led by rising output in Vaca Muerta, a huge shale play in northern Patagonia. Shale oil production shot up 27.7% year over year to 703,473 b/d in September, the report showed.

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

17

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

November 4, 2026

Vaca Muerta is expected to drive Argentina's total oil output to 1 million b/d by the end of this year and 1.7 million b/d in 2030, according to forecasts by the national government and the Argentinian Chamber of Hydrocarbons Exploration and Production, an industry group.
Production growth is expected to pick up pace as an under construction export pipeline and terminal comes into operation, with the first 180,000 b/d of capacity starting in January 2027. This will continue to increase takeaway capacity from the play as the Vaca Muerta Oil Sur system is scaled up to 550,000 b/d in 2028, with the potential of increasing that to 700,000 b/d with pumping units. All this capacity will be available for export, given that Argentina consumes an average of 500,000 b/d of its production.
Gas exports tumble Meanwhile, gas exports fell 29% year over year and 36% from
August, the report found. The decline came even as production rose 3% year over year
to 142.8 million cu m/d in September and fell 8.1% from 155.4 million in August.
Over the same period, unconventional gas production -- most of it comes from Vaca Muerta -- rose 11.2% year over year to 98.6 million cu m/d in September and was down 9.3% from 108.7 million in August, the data showed.
The increase in gas exports is expected to take longer than that of oil, given the larger and costlier investments required to expand cross-border pipeline capacity to neighboring countries and to build LNG liquefaction capacity. A consortium of gas producers, led in different phases by YPF and BP-backed Pan American Energy, is working on projects to build as much as 24 million mt/year of LNG export capacity, starting in 2027 and reaching full capacity in the early 2030s.
With the additional export capacity, Vaca Muerta's gas production is expected to reach 281 million cu m/d by 2030, according to the industry forecast.
-- Charles Newbery
Argentina's Capex to buy stakes in Loma Negra and La Yesera oil blocks
Acquisition deal is with YPF
Deal will boost its ownership in the fields
Capex is expanding into Vaca Muerta
Argentine oil producer Capex said late Nov. 3 it reached a deal to buy 35% stakes in the Loma Negra and La Yesera blocks, aiming to boost conventional oil production and help finance its first drilling in the Vaca Muerta shale play.
The company will buy the holdings for $6 million from statebacked YPF, the country's biggest oil producer, it said in a securities filing.
Capex already owned 37.5% stakes in both blocks, meaning that the deal will take its total ownership to 72.5%, according to company data.

Loma Negra and La Yesera are in Río Negro, a province east and south of Neuquén, where most developments are focused on Vaca Muerta, a huge play driving the country's oil and gas production and export growth.
YPF has been selling all its conventional blocks to focus on Vaca Muerta, which is opening up opportunities for smaller companies like Capex to squeeze more out of the maturing conventional fields using tertiary techniques.
On Oct. 21, Capex CEO Adolfo Storni told Platts that the company will use some of the proceeds from its conventional output to finance its expansion into Vaca Muerta, with a target of reaching 20,000 b/d over the next few years. That will double its current production of 20,000 b/d from conventional blocks.
Platts is part of S&P Global Energy. -- Charles Newbery
Brazil's Prio sets oil, gas production record in Oct on production well, maintenance work
Frade field MUP7 well production at 5,000 b/d
Well restarts at Bravo
Albacora Leste field production grows
Brazilian independent producer Prio pushed monthly output to a fresh record high in October after starting output from a new production well and completing repairs and maintenance work at several offshore fields, according to a company production report.
Prio pumped a record average of 196,375 b/d of oil equivalent in October, an increase of 1.9% from 192,563 boe/d in September, the company said Nov. 3 after markets closed. That topped the previous monthly output record of 196,260 boe/d set in July. October's production also more than doubled the 89,584 boe/d produced in October 2025.
The record-setting production growth was largely driven by incremental gains across Prio's portfolio of mature offshore assets, where the company has ramped up efforts to increase output and operational efficiency by modernizing equipment and conducting maintenance work. In addition, Prio has boosted nearfield exploration activity to tap isolated reservoirs at the Frade and Peregrino fields.
The Valente cluster, for example, continued to benefit from the start of the MUP7 production well at the Frade field, which pumped first oil on Sept. 5, according to Prio. Initial output from the well was about 5,000 b/d. Prio also plans to add a second production well tapping another isolated reservoir at the field before the end of 2026.
Prio also plans to start work on two injection wells at the Wahoo field in 2026, which pumped first oil in March and is also part of the Valente cluster. The injection wells were part of the initial development plan for Wahoo.
The Valente production cluster pumped 70,603 b/d of oil and 4,071 boe/d of natural gas in October, mixed when compared with

the 69,177 b/d of oil and 4,885 boe/d of natural gas registered in September, Prio said. October's oil and gas output also more than doubled from 31,566 boe/d in October 2025, before the Wahoo field started production.
Prio owns 100% of output from the Frade and Wahoo fields. Output at the Peregrino heavy oil field, meanwhile, remained steady month over month in October, Prio said. Peregrino is Prio's top production asset, but produces no natural gas. Peregrino produced 82,373 b/d in October, down slightly from 82,381 b/d in September, Prio said. October's output also was up from 18,479 b/d in October 2025, when production was restarted after a regulator-ordered shutdown. Prio currently owns an 80% operating stake in Peregrino, but expects to complete its deal to acquire the remaining 20% equity stake in the field from Norway's Equinor in the fourth quarter.
Albacora Leste, Bravo cluster gains The Albacora Leste field and Bravo production cluster also
both registered solid month-over-month gains, according to Prio.
Operational efficiency has steadily improved at Albacora Leste after a series of equipment upgrades and maintenance work, including projects on board the FPSO Forte floating production, storage and offloading vessel in late August and early September, according to Prio. Albacora Leste produced 25,091 b/d of oil and 489 boe/d of gas in October, up from 23,040 b/d and 199 boe/d in September. October's production also rose from 24,843 boe/d in October 2025.
Prio owns a 90% operating stake in the field, while Spanish-Chinese joint venture Repsol Sinopec retains the remaining 10%.
The completion of repairs to the OGX-44HP production, meanwhile, lifted output from the Bravo cluster, Prio said. The well restarted production on Oct. 23. Prio owns 100% of the Bravo cluster, which includes the Polvo and Tubarao Martelo fields.
The Bravo cluster produced 13,749 b/d in October, up from 12,881 b/d in September, Prio said. October's output, meanwhile, was down from 14,696 b/d in October 2025.
Despite the higher oil output in October, crude oil offloads decreased slightly amid offloads by partner companies, Prio said. In October, Repsol Sinopec offloaded 491,600 barrels from the Albacora Leste.
Prio offloaded 5.149 million barrels in October, down 5.2% from 5.429 million barrels in September, Prio said. October's offloads, however, were nearly five times the 1.122 million barrels offloaded in October 2025.
The Peregrino field was the top source of oil offloads in October at 2.255 million barrels, up from 1.683 million barrels in September, Prio said. The Valente cluster was second at 1.852 million barrels in October, followed by the Albacora Leste field at 895,598 barrels and the Bravo cluster at 146,972 barrels.

Matador raises output guidance on strong well performance
Q3 output reaches 230,470 boe/d, tops forecast
Company expects mid-single-digit growth in 2027
New wells exceed expectations across basin
Matador Resources raised its full-year 2026 production guidance after third-quarter output topped expectations, and said it could deliver "mid-single-digit growth or better" in 2027 based on current commodity pricing and oilfield service costs.
The Delaware Basin producer reported Q3 production of 230,470 boe/d in its Nov. 3 earnings release, up 10% year over year and 3% above the midpoint of its guidance. Oil output averaged 132,019 b/d, above guidance of 128,500-130,500 b/d, while natural gas production was 590.7 MMcf/d. The company attributed the beat primarily to outperformance of new wells turned to sales during the quarter.
Matador lifted its full-year production guidance to 223,000224,000 boe/d from 218,500-223,500 boe/d, and its oil guidance to 128,750-129,250 b/d from 127,500-129,000 b/d. The company said the new midpoint represents 7% production growth from 2025.
For Q4, Matador guided to 238,000-242,000 boe/d, including record oil production of about 137,000 b/d, or roughly 4% sequential growth. The Paloma and Ridge Runner acquisitions, which closed in October, are expected to contribute about 10,000 boe/d in the quarter, 57% of it oil, the company said.
On a Nov. 4 earnings call, CFO Chris Calvert said it was too soon to outline a 2027 budget.
"Obviously we've seen oil price change drastically in the last 48 hours," Calvert said. "You're going to have oilfield service that is somewhat following activity that could be based off of that oil price change, and so I think it's a little early to comment on what we think a capital program could look like in 2027."
Platts assessed the WTI Cushing front-month contract at $96.09/b at close Nov. 3, down from $104.12/b Oct. 30. Platts Dated Brent has also fallen in recent days, to $105.19/b Nov. 3 from $127.98/b Oct. 26, as the market weighed the potential for a diplomatic resolution to the US-Israel war with Iran.
Realized prices Crude oil futures dipped further in European midday trading
Nov. 4 following the outcome of the US midterm elections. Democrats won sweeping gains to overtake both the House of Representatives and the Senate, as well as many statewide offices, after US President Donald Trump acknowledged the impact of the Iran conflict on the outcome.
At 12:20 pm London time (1220 GMT), the ICE January Brent futures contract was trading $1.53/barrel lower from the previous settle at $102.07/b, while the NYMEX December WTI contract fell $1.04/b to $94.99/b.
Matador Founder and CEO Joseph Foran said vendor costs tend to follow crude lower. "If oil prices go down, the vendor prices tend to go down with it, and they just adjust," he said.

GeoPark plans to restart 150 Venezuela wells in 2027; accelerate drilling in Argentina
Venezuela contract effective, operational takeover by year-end
Vaca Muerta factory-mode drilling scheduled for December
$600 million-$700 million in financing available for expansion
GeoPark said Nov. 4 that it plans to reactivate up to 150 oil wells in Venezuela in 2027 while accelerating drilling in Argentina's Vaca Muerta shale formation, as the company expands production beyond its traditional Colombian operations.
The company expects to assume operations at Venezuela's Bare Block before the end of 2026, following the effectiveness of its 25year production participation contract with PDVSA, while factorymode drilling in Argentina is scheduled to begin in December, management said during the company's Q3 earnings call.
In Venezuela, GeoPark plans to reactivate between 100 and 150 wells during 2027, representing approximately one-third of the between 300 and 450 reactivations planned through 2030, chief operating officer Martín Terrado said.
The company also expects to conduct about 25 workovers next year, while new drilling is scheduled to begin in 2028, with approximately 30 wells planned through 2030.
"For the first year, we are going to be doing reactivation," Terrado said, adding that the initial program would primarily involve operating expenditures.
GeoPark has already evaluated between 300 and 400 of the block's 1,100 existing wells to identify reactivation candidates, management said.
More than half of the critical service contracts required to assume operations have been awarded, with the remainder expected to be awarded within two to three weeks, Terrado said.
The company plans to begin operations with one or two rigs before gradually increasing activity.
GeoPark estimates that Bare contains approximately 16 billion barrels of oil in place, with potential cumulative production of around 600 million barrels over 25 years.
CEO Felipe Bayón reiterated a gross production figure of between 15,000b/d and 20,000 b/d for the block, although he did not specify when those volumes could be reached.

-- Eamonn Brennan

The company has incorporated technical information from those wells into its development plans and recently completed a 3D seismic survey to support future drilling, Bayón said.
"Vaca Muerta is going to be a highly intensive capex development over the next few years," Bayón said.
GeoPark has also completed initial connections to regional oil and natural gas pipelines, reducing its reliance on trucking and improving its ability to handle additional production.

ICE front-month Singapore gasoline swap open interest falls 8% on month in Oct
Singapore 92 RON gasoline swaps open interest declines on month
Near-term liquidity crunched on easing supply concerns, winter demand lull
Total open interest for front-month November Singapore gasoline derivative contracts traded on the Intercontinental Exchange in October fell 14.35% month over month to 24.192 million barrels, the latest ICE data showed on Nov. 3.
Open interest for the front-month November Singapore 92 RON gasoline swaps contract fell 8.22% month over month to 16.206 million barrels in October.
Near-term market liquidity deteriorated amid easing supply concerns following China's allocation of 5.65 million metric tons of clean oil product export quotas in its fourth batch of the year, lifting the 2026 cumulative total by 1.9% year over year to 42.19 million mt, Platts reported Oct. 29.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the frontmonth Singapore 92 RON gasoline time spread at an average of $6.20/barrel in October, narrowing 35.65% from September,

demonstrating weakness in sentiment surrounding the complex across October.
Additionally, the FOB Singapore 92 RON gasoline cash differential averaged a premium of $10.30/b in October, widening from $8.76/b in September, Platts data showed.
US retail gasoline prices, often used as a proxy for demand, eased to an average of $4.449/gallon in October, retreating from a four-month high average of $4.491/gal in September, according to data from the US Energy Information Administration.
Softening pump prices reflect the seasonal demand lull as driving demand typically wanes while winter approaches.
Indonesia -- the region's largest importer of gasoline -- also saw its currency weaken in October, which also dampened demand for the product.
By the end of October, the Indonesian rupiah traded at 18,140 rupiah/$1, compared to 17,974 rupiah/$1 at the start of the month.
Indonesia's headline inflation eased to 3.23% year over year in October, while prices rose 0.22% month over month, latest data from Statistics Indonesia showed. The annualized October inflation reading fell below the market's anticipated level of a 3.29% increase, and retreated from September's three-month high print at a 3.28% increase.
Meanwhile, open interest for the front-month Singapore 92 RON gasoline swap against ICE Brent futures crack fell 24.56% month over month to 7.986 million barrels.
The Platts front-month FOB Singapore 92 RON gasoline swap crack spread against front-month Brent swap averaged $21.59/b in October, narrowing 21.70% from $27.57/b in September, Platts data showed.
The mid-term view softened month over month. Platts assessed the front-quarter Q1/Q2 Asian 92 RON gasoline swaps time spread at an average of plus $5.80/b over October, narrowing from an average spread of plus $13.09/b the previous month.
